Through the wall, she could hear his voice — muffled at first, then clearer when he shifted closer to his laptop. She tried, with the small ferocity of someone practicing trust, to do nothing but intervene. Just listen. Just let him have his people.

Kai hadn’t done a group call in months.

Not because he didn’t want to.  
 Because he didn’t know how to explain the shape of his life now — or the shape of his absence.

But parallel living had a way of making silence feel dishonest, so he sat at his desk, laptop angled just so, and typed into the group chat:

Jump on? Need a sanity check.

Three dots appeared almost immediately.

**NICS:** oh my god he LIVES  
 **MARIE:** yesss wait i need my mug  
 **ALEX:** Confirmed alive?  
 **ANNE:** typing… stopped

Before he could rethink any of it, the call connected.

Marie’s face popped up first — oversized sweater, soft lighting, hair committing to chaos.

“Kai\!” she squealed. “You look—wait, are you *glowing*? Is that… joy? Ew.”

He smiled despite himself. “Hi, Marie.”

Nics slammed into the frame mid-chew. “Explain. Start at the betrayal.”

Alex joined a second later, leaning against his headboard, the picture of controlled concern. “No pressure,” he said, which of course made it worse.

Kai cleared his throat. “I’m sorry I went dark.”

“Dark?” Nics scoffed. “You went *witness protection*. No memes. No ‘I’m dying’ voice notes. No ‘I regret my life choices’ at 2 a.m. Nothing.”

Marie nodded solemnly. “We considered a manhunt. Or a seance.”

Alex watched him with that steady, infuriating gentleness. “We weren’t sure if you needed space or if you were drowning.”

Kai looked down at his hands, then back up. “…I’m okay,” he said. “Or getting there.”

Nics narrowed her eyes. “Is this about her?”

On the couch in the other apartment, Ileana’s fingers tightened around her mug.

Marie made a tiny sound; Alex’s expression stilled.

Kai nodded once. “Yeah. It is.”

“DROP. THE. LORE,” Nics demanded.

“She’s back,” he said.

On-screen, three reactions crashed into each other:

Marie’s hands flew to her mouth.  
 Nics choked on whatever she was eating.  
 Alex blinked twice, sharply.

“Back where?” Nics said. “Emotionally, spiritually, geographically, *scent-wise*? LIST THE DIMENSIONS.”

Kai hesitated. “Across the hall.”

Marie actually shrieked.

Alex straightened. “Sorry—wait. *What?*”

“She lives right there,” Kai said, gesturing behind him. “Twenty steps.”

Nics made a noise like a kettle having an existential crisis. “OH. MY. GOD. You domesticated a *myth*.”

Marie stared at him like he’d confessed to moving in with a dragon. “Hold on. Hold ON. You *moved*? You actually moved hundreds of miles to be with her?”

Kai opened his mouth, closed it, then surrendered. “Well… her parents asked.”

There was a perfect, round silence.

Then:

“HER PARENTS?\!” Nics yelped.

“The *actual* parents?” Marie yelped over her.

Alex exhaled through his nose. “Honestly? That… tracks.”

Kai lifted a hand in a small, useless gesture. “And I wasn’t going to let a chance like that slip by.”

Marie slapped her palm down on the table just out of frame. “THIS IS INSANE. BEAUTIFUL. BUT INSANE.”

“You’ve always been decisive when it counts,” Alex said quietly.

Nics leaned closer to the camera, eyes narrowed. “So. How’s the place? Water pressure. Doors that close. Rent. I need the logistics of this insanity.”

“It’s small,” Kai answered. “Light’s good. Rent’s… okay.”

Marie squinted. “How okay?”

He rubbed the back of his neck. “…manageable.”

Nics pointed at the screen like she’d caught him in a crime. “Translation: financially reckless for love.”

“Not reckless,” Kai said. “Intentional.”

Marie groaned. “That’s WORSE. That’s rom-com worse.”

“You moved your whole existence like a suitcase someone asked you to set by the window,” Nics declared.

Kai laughed, helpless.

Through the wall, Ileana pressed the rim of the mug to her lips to keep her mouth from doing something unsteady.

“She’s… tired,” he went on. “Trying. She’s doing her rotations. Reading my letters. One a day.”

Marie’s eyes softened. “She’s really reading them?”

He nodded. “Sometimes she cries. Sometimes she falls asleep halfway through. Sometimes she throws a pillow at me and says ‘this bit is unfair.’ But she’s reading.”

“And?” Alex asked.

“And she falls asleep on my couch after thirty-hour shifts,” he said. “I cover her with a blanket. She drools on my hoodie.”

Nics clutched her chest. “Romance. Is. ALIVE.”

“And she picked my bedding,” he added, almost as an afterthought.

Alex actually jolted. “She picked your *sheets*?”

“She said I have ‘owl aesthetic.’”

Marie whispered, “She’s right, though.”

Kai shrugged. “We’re figuring out the boring bits. Mornings. Meals. Laundry. Boundaries. It’s… slower. Less myth. More life.”

“You sound grounded,” Alex said.

“I feel grounded,” Kai replied.

On the other side of the wall, Ileana closed her eyes, letting the words settle somewhere that hadn’t felt grounded in a long time.

A notification blinked.

**ANNE joined the call.**

The entire atmosphere shifted — not hostile, just tighter. Like the group had collectively inhaled and forgotten how to exhale.

Anne’s camera came on. Soft lamplight. Hair tucked behind one ear. Eyes tired, careful, too sharp.

“Hi,” she said.

“Hey,” Kai answered.

Marie froze mid-sip.  
 Alex straightened.  
 Nics silently mouthed, *oh, shit*.

“I heard she’s back,” Anne said.

“She is.”

“And you’re… alright?” she asked. Not jealous. Not accusatory. Just… worried.

“I’m better,” he said. “We’re trying.”

Anne nodded once. “Good.”

The tension loosened — not gone, but less jagged.

“This is so awkward I’m physically aging,” Nics muttered.

Marie kicked her under the table.

Alex cleared his throat. “Kai… you disappeared.”

Kai looked down. “…I know.”

“You didn’t check in,” Marie said, voice as gentle as it was uncompromising.

“I know.”

“You’re picking up her habit of disappearing,” Nics added, pointing a chopstick at the screen. “Which, rude.”

Alex cut in, voice lighter but no less true. “But you always come back.”

Something in Kai’s chest pulled tight and then let go.

Anne’s expression shifted — something like reluctant relief. “That’s what matters.”

He nodded. “Thank you.”

Nics clapped once. “Okay. GROUND RULES.”

“Oh god,” Kai muttered.

“One,” she announced, “if you vanish again with no warning, I’m stealing a kidney.”

“Left one,” Marie said automatically.

“He needs the left one,” Alex countered. “Take the right.”

“Two,” Nics continued, “if she hurts you—”

“Nic—”

“—we will respectfully mind our business,” she finished, “because both of you are disasters who love each other, and that’s above my paygrade.”

Marie lifted her mug. “Hear, hear.”

Alex nodded. “You look happier than you’ve been in a long time. That’s enough for me.”

Anne sighed, the sound low and tired. “Try not to implode.”

Kai huffed out a small laugh. “I’ll do my best.”

Nics made a sound that was half cheer, half exhale. “Dinner,” she said. “You’re doing a dinner. You absolute romantic.”

“Not romantic,” Kai corrected, slipping into his neutral, businesslike cadence. “Serious. Reservation made. Steakhouse. Proper.”

Alex whistled. “When a Kai reservation drops, it means tectonic plates are moving.”

“Okay, but for real,” Marie said. “What do you want from us? For us to welcome her? Just listen? Are we being moderated by a man who manages other people’s guilt for a living?”

“You,” Kai said simply, and somehow the word felt bigger than it was. “I want honesty. I want you to speak. Test me if you must. But no theatrics. If you care, come. If you can’t, say so. I’ll respect it.”

There was a complicated silence — not empty, but heavy with shared history. Each of them had held some piece of him while everything was falling apart. The idea of adding another piece to that ledger meant something.

Then Anne said, measured but brittle, “I can’t promise warmth.”

“You don’t have to,” Kai replied. “Neither do I. Just sit with it, at least. We’ll meet. Talk properly. That’s all I’m asking.”

The texture of the call softened. People began making small, adult bargains with themselves.

Nics leaned back. “Fine. But if this turns into a rom-com, I’m demanding commission.”

Alex snorted. “If it turns into a rom-com, it’s because Kai finally learns how to fold a fitted sheet.”

The laugh that escaped Ileana’s throat surprised her. It was small, cracked at the edges, but still a laugh. It startled her enough that she covered her mouth, as if she’d broken some rule by letting herself feel something simple.

“Okay,” they agreed at last. “Dinner. This week. Proper conversation. No ambush.”

Anne’s sign-off was curt. She said nothing more. Her square went dark.

One by one, the others dropped off the call.  
 The last thing to vanish was Alex’s steady gaze.

Then the screen was just his reflection.

He sat there for a few seconds in the quiet, letting his shoulders register the weight they’d been carrying and the small fraction of it that had just been set down.

Then —

A soft knock.

He turned in his chair.

Ileana stood in the doorway, damp hair, bare feet, wearing his hoodie like she’d always owned it. Her face was careful in the way someone’s face gets when the stakes are suddenly very real.

“You’re done?” she asked.

“Yeah,” he said, and the smile he gave her wasn’t polished; it was tired and real. “Come in.”

She stepped inside like it was the most natural thing in the world.

Maybe — in some version of their life that hadn’t splintered and rerouted — it always had been.

He patted the empty cushion beside him.  
 She crossed the room and sat, knees brushing, both of them looking at the closed laptop like it was a door they’d just opened together.

“They’ll come,” he said quietly.

“I heard,” she whispered. “I’m… terrified.”

“Me too,” he admitted.

She blinked, startled by the honesty.

“We’ll do it anyway,” he added. “With care.”

She nodded, throat tight, fingers curling in the hem of the hoodie.

For now, there would be flights and reservations and a table in another city.

But tonight, there was this: the kettle ticking as it cooled in the next room, the faint smell of pastry and plant soil, the warmth of his knee against hers, and the knowledge that the story — theirs, and his with them — was no longer being told in separate rooms.

It would have to be spoken aloud now.  
 Around a table.  
 With witnesses.

And for the first time, the thought didn’t feel like a verdict.

It felt like a beginning.

# **INTERLUDE  III**

*The Godling Learns Her Name*

The Citadel was awake.

Not loudly — divine realms never woke with noise — but in the subtle way temperature shifts before dawn, or how the sea adjusts itself when the moon leans closer. The walls, once sharp with vigilance, hummed at a lower frequency now. Softer. As if the realm had learned how to exhale.

At the center of the chamber floated the godling: a small, luminous thing with no true shape, more feeling than form. It pulsed in slow waves, drifting over the polished stone like a spark deciding whether to become a flame.

It had never been this curious before.

**“She is back,”** the godling whispered, though whispering for it sounded like a ripple of warm light.  
 **“She stays. She touched him with warmth.”**

The entity, older than names and more patient than most gods had ever been, watched from its vantage at the balcony of unmoving stars.

“Yes,” it said, voice low, carved of stone and moonlit reason. “She stays.”

The godling spun in a delighted arc, scattering flecks of light that clung to the air before dissolving.  
 **“She makes him feel… steady.”**

“Steady is not the same as safe,” the entity corrected.

But its voice lacked the warning it once carried. The Citadel walls no longer braced for collapse each time the human world shifted. Something had changed — subtly, but undeniably.

The godling drifted toward the great reflective floor where Kai’s divine echo rested: a silent, seated form shaped of light, folded paradox, and memory. The echo did not move, but it warmed faintly when the godling approached, the way a sleeping body recognizes familiar footsteps.

**“He is soft when he thinks of her,”** the godling said matter-of-factly.

“Yes,” the entity said again.  
 There was no point pretending otherwise.

The godling circled the echo’s shoulder, pulsing gently. **“She is soft too. But sharp at the edges. Like new fruit. Or something alive that learned to be afraid.”**

The entity considered this.

“She is human,” it said finally. “They grow around their wounds.”

The godling tilted like a curious bird.  
 **“Do gods grow?”**

The entity paused.

And then: “Only when forced.”

The godling drifted away from the echo, warm light trailing behind like a child leaving fingerprints on glass. It hovered by the Citadel’s northern arch — the one that mirrored the seam between realms.

Through the shimmering surface, it could feel Ileana sleeping.  
 Her breath, uneven at first, then softening as she curled closer to Kai during the night.  
 Her exhaustion like a warm shadow.  
 Her fear like a fading outline.  
 Her presence like a new gravitational pull.

The godling reached a small tendril of light toward the seam.

Not touching.  
 Just sensing.

**“What is her name?”** it asked suddenly.

The entity looked up.

It had not expected this question.

“You have never asked that before,” it said.

The godling pulsed brighter.  
 **“I want to know her. Not just her warmth.”**

The entity watched the realm itself respond — the arch glowing faintly in recognition, the air shifting as though something in the Citadel leaned forward with interest.

“Her name,” the entity said slowly, “is Ileana.”

The godling repeated it in its strange, luminous cadence:  
 **“Ilee…ana.”**

The realm hummed.  
 The echo warmed.  
 Somewhere close to the floor, old inscriptions reassembled their vowels.

The godling spun like a delighted ember.  
 **“Ileana,”** it said again.  
 **“Ileana.”**

The entity clasped its hands behind its back.

“That is not a word you should bind yourself to lightly.”

The godling paused mid-spin.  
 **“Why?”**

“Names,” the entity said, “are anchors. Even for gods.”

The godling thought about that.  
 Or tried to.  
 Thinking was still new to it.

Then, softly:

**“She anchored him.”**

The entity’s gaze drifted downward, toward the seam between worlds where the sleeping silhouettes of Kai and Ileana blurred into one soft shape of breath and bone and trust.

“Yes,” it said. “She did.”

And for the first time, the entity did not say this with hesitation.  
 Or caution.  
 Or analysis.

It said it like truth.  
 Simple.  
 Settled.  
 Accepted.

The godling drifted closer to the seam, emboldened.

**“Can I meet her?”** it asked.

“Not yet,” the entity replied. “You are too young. You carry too much of him and too little of yourself.”

The godling dimmed slightly.  
 **“But I want to see her.”**

“You already do,” the entity murmured. “Every time he breathes easier.”

The godling pulsed.  
 **“She makes him warm.”**

“Yes,” the entity said, almost smiling. “She does.”

The Citadel glowed faintly — warm at its edges, soft at its corners — not reborn, but rebalanced.

As the godling whispered her name again, the realm listened.

**Ileana.**

And the name fit into the architecture like a gently placed stone —  
 not intrusive, not demanding,  
 but quietly exactly where it was meant to be.

# **CHAPTER XXVIII** 

*The Flight*

The morning arrived before either of them felt ready for it.

The alarm went off quietly, a soft vibration against the nightstand. Kai reached for it immediately, muting the sound before it could feel like an intrusion. He sat on the edge of the bed for a moment — elbows on knees, head bowed — gathering himself in the stillness.

Across the hall, he heard the faint shuffle of Ileana waking.  
 A cup placed gently on the counter.  
 The tap running.  
 Soft footsteps.

Their worlds had become parallel routines, two apartments with a shared gravity, and this morning held a weight even silence didn’t know how to carry.

He knocked once — soft, a question more than sound.

She opened her door already dressed, hair still damp from the shower, hoodie swapped for something cleaner but just as borrowed-looking. Her eyes met his, a quiet tangle of fear and determination.

“Ready?” he asked.

“No,” she whispered honestly.

He nodded. “Me neither.”

They left anyway.

The taxi ride was a landscape of half-formed sentences.

“You don’t have to—”  
 “I want to.”  
 “But the cost—”  
 “Stop.”  
 “That’s not—”  
 “I said stop.”

Not harsh, not scolding.  
 Just a boundary said with a kind of gentleness that almost hurt.

At the airport, morning light spilled through the atrium windows like something trying too hard to be comforting. The lines moved quickly; the crowds were manageable; the entire place had the energy of a day that didn’t yet know it would matter.

He handled the tickets.  
 She handled the small talk with the check-in staff.  
 Together it felt strangely normal — practiced even.

At security she hesitated before stepping through, breath catching.  
 He noticed immediately.

“Hey.”  
 His voice was quiet.  
 Not asking — anchoring.

She exhaled and walked through the metal frame.  
 No alarm.  
 No interruption.  
 Just the muted beep of permission.

He smiled at her — small, barely-there, enough.

At the Gate

They found seats near the window, overlooking a runway blurred by heat and morning haze. The plane they would board sat like something waiting to be confessed to.

She hugged her knees to her chest.

“I don’t know how they’ll look at me,” she said softly.

Kai didn’t pretend to know the answer.  
 He didn’t give her empty reassurance.  
 He didn’t give her half-promises or comforting guesses.

Instead, he said the one thing that belonged fully to truth:

“I’ll be with you.”

She swallowed hard.  
 “That shouldn’t make it easier.”

“It does,” he said simply.

She looked down at her hands, then at the boarding pass crumpling slightly between her fingers.

“This feels unreal.”

“I know.”

“Like I’m walking into the consequences of a version of myself I don’t remember being.”

He tilted his head gently. “You remember. You just don’t want to look at her.”

Her breath caught.  
 He didn’t retract the line.  
 He meant it kindly.

Boarding

When their group was called, he waited for her to stand first.  
 She didn’t move.

“Kai,” she whispered. “I’m scared.”

He offered her his hand.

Not demanded.  
 Not insisted.  
 Just offered.

She took it.

Her hand was cold; his was warm; the contrast made both of them wince in the same moment.

They walked down the jet bridge like two people moving toward something irreversible.

The plane lifted into the air with a shudder, and the world below softened into clouds and shrinking fields.

She leaned her forehead against the window, watching the horizon blur. Kai sat beside her, quiet, unnervingly steady. His hands rested on his thighs, not fidgeting, not stilled — just present.

Halfway through the climb, she closed her eyes.  
 Her breath hitched once.  
 Twice.

Then:

“Kai?”  
 “Mm?”  
 “Can I…” She hesitated. “Can I stay close?”

He turned slightly toward her, shoulder brushing hers.  
 “You don’t have to ask.”

She shifted — slowly, carefully — until her head rested on his shoulder.  
 No dramatics.  
 No trembling confessions.  
 Just a tired girl leaning into someone who knew how to hold still.

His arm moved almost instinctively around her, drawing her in, steady and warm. She exhaled for the first time since dawn.

The hum of the engine swallowed the rest of the world.

Her breathing evened out, soft and shallow, syncing with the rhythm of his chest.

She fell asleep like that — curled into him, pulse slowing.  
 He didn’t move.  
 Not once.

He stared out the window, watching the clouds pass, holding her as if stillness was the only language that mattered.

She woke as the plane began its descent, groggy and disoriented.

“How long was I out?” she murmured.

“About an hour.”

“Did I drool?”

“A little.”

She groaned into her hands. “Oh my God.”

“It was endearing.”

“Please stop.”

He didn’t.  
 He just looked at her with a softness that seemed to undo parts of her she thought were sealed forever.

Out the window, the city where his friends lived — where their past splintered, where tonight’s dinner waited like a trial — came into view.

Concrete.  
 Glass.  
 The familiar skyline of a place they had both left for different reasons, now returning for the same one.

Her breath stilled.

“Kai.”

“Hm?”

“Do you think this is a mistake?”

He didn’t hesitate.

“No.”

She wished she had his certainty.  
 Or his calm.  
 Or even his stubbornness.

But all she had was his shoulder and the faint warmth of where her cheek had rested.

And for the moment — that was enough.

When the wheels touched down, she felt her stomach drop in a way that had nothing to do with altitude.

They stood, retrieved their bags, and stepped out into a terminal filled with voices and motion and none of the emotional space they needed.

At the taxi bay, as the sky dipped into the first hints of evening, she wrapped her arms around herself.

“Kai,” she whispered, barely audible. “If I break—”

“You won’t,” he said.

“And if I do?”

“I’ll be there when you put the pieces back together.”

She blinked rapidly, steadying herself.

“You don’t have to—”

He shook his head.  
 “I want to.”

The taxi pulled up.  
 He opened the door for her.  
 She stepped inside.

The city waited.

Dinner waited.

Consequences waited.

But beside her, seatbelt clicking into place, was someone who didn’t flinch from impact.

And for the first time in months, she felt the faint, impossible warmth of something like hope.

# **CHAPTER XXIX** 

*The Dinner*

The taxi ride to the restaurant felt shorter than it should have been.  
 Too short for the questions she hadn’t asked,  
 too short for the answers he hadn’t offered,  
 too short for the fear that had been building in her chest like a storm with no wind.

Kai sat beside her with one hand braced lightly on the center console, the other resting on his thigh. Calm. Measured. Nothing in his posture said panic, but nothing said confidence either. He looked like someone walking into a room where the past waited with a chair pulled out.

She didn’t know how he did it.

“They’ll be kind,” he said softly, without looking at her.  
 “They’ll be fair.”

“That’s worse,” she whispered.

He almost smiled. “Yeah. I know.”

The taxi slowed. Street lights blinked across the window — warm golds, soft reflections bending over car hoods and puddles.

The restaurant loomed ahead:  
 dark wood, tall windows, the sort of place whose lighting warmed the skin and hid the shadows under tired eyes.

A place where big conversations happened because you couldn’t run without disturbing someone else’s soup.

He paid the driver before she could reach for her wallet.  
 She didn’t argue.  
 Her voice didn’t trust itself yet.

## 

The hostess greeted them with a professional smile.  
 Reservation style smile: polite, impressed, discreet.

“Good evening. Table for six? The private alcove?”

Kai nodded.

She led them through the restaurant — the lighting dimming as they passed through the polished interior, until they reached a corner space set slightly apart from the main dining room. Round table. White linen. Water glasses that caught the light like faint constellations.

They were early.

But not the first.

Alex stood as soon as he saw them.

Marie lifted her hand in a small wave.

Nics nearly fell out of her chair with how hard she flailed.

Anne didn’t move — she only acknowledged their arrival with a tight, polite nod that felt sharpened around the edges.

Kai pulled out a chair for Ileana, and she sat with careful breath, every movement deliberate and quiet.

He took the seat beside her — close enough that she could feel the faint heat of his arm, but not touching.

A waiter slipped in like a ghost.

“Good evening. May I bring the wine list?”

Before Kai could say a word, Nics leaned forward.

“Before ANYTHING,” she said, pointing at the waiter as if cross-examining him, “please confirm: did this man—” she jabbed her thumb toward Kai— “book the pretentious Wagyu steaks? The ones that cost like half a semester’s tuition?”

The waiter blinked. “We do have several Wagyu options—”

Kai sighed. “We’re here for a serious conversation. I wasn’t going to bring them to Jollibee.”

Alex snorted. “Of course he didn’t cheap out.”

Marie smiled, eyes warm. “This is very you.”

Ileana felt her cheeks heat. She had forgotten this part — how his generosity wasn’t performance, but reflex. How he used intention like currency.

Menus were handed around.  
 Nics made dramatic noises at the prices.  
 Alex made dry comments under his breath.  
 Marie tried to be polite but kept peeking at every dish description like she was trying to decode it.

Anne didn’t open her menu immediately.  
 She was watching Ileana.

Not with hostility.  
 Not with disdain.  
 But with a kind of careful study, like someone trying to determine whether a shape in the distance was a threat or a memory.

## 

One by one, orders were made.

Alex: “Ribeye. Medium rare.”  
 Marie: “Lobster, thank you.”  
 Nics: “Whatever the most sinful thing on this menu is. Surprise me.”  
 Anne: “Salmon. No butter.”  
 Kai: “Wagyu striploin.”

Ileana blinked down at her menu.  
 “Um… filet mignon. Medium well—”

Kai turned to her gently.

“You prefer medium,” he murmured.

She froze.  
 “Do I?”

He nodded with certainty, then turned back to the waiter.

“She’ll have it medium.”

Nics slapped the table.  
 “I KNEW IT. I KNEW YOU’D REMEMBER HER FOOD PREFERENCES BEFORE YOUR OWN.”

Marie added, “He forgets his birthday. But her steak? Never.”

“Birthdays are irrelevant,” Kai said. “Steak temperatures aren’t.”

The table dissolved into laughter.  
 Even Anne’s mouth twitched, though she suppressed it immediately.

Ileana tried not to smile.  
 Tried.  
 Failed.

Warm bread arrived — fragrant, soft, whipped butter sprinkled with sea salt.

Conversation loosened, little by little.

Marie told a story about a disastrous shift.  
 Nics reenacted a date gone wrong.  
 Alex mentioned training with Kai and muttered something about “man lifts like he’s punishing gravity.”

Kai listened.  
 Added comments where needed.  
 Protected the silence where it mattered.

Ileana absorbed everything like a creature thawing under gentle heat.

And then — as if testing herself — she spoke.

Soft.  
 Intentional.

“I’m not running.”

The table quieted.

Not sharply.  
 Not accusingly.  
 Just noticing.

Marie’s shoulders eased.  
 Alex’s expression softened.  
 Nics blinked, genuinely touched.

Anne looked down.

A small, quiet earthquake.

Then the food arrived.

Plates were placed with reverence — gleaming cuts of steak, lobster shells shining, salmon resting in gentle herbs.

Nics moaned dramatically.  
 Marie elbowed her.  
 Alex cut into his steak like the world was suddenly manageable.

It was almost normal.

Almost.

Then Anne put down her fork.

The table stilled.

“I’m glad the food is good,” she said softly.

Ileana’s stomach dropped.

Anne looked at her fully for the first time.

“But food doesn’t fix everything.”

Ileana nodded. “No. It doesn’t.”

Anne’s jaw worked.  
 Emotion rising and sinking.  
 Controlled.  
 Measured.

“You hurt us,” she said.  
 Not an indictment.  
 A truth.

Ileana swallowed. “I know.”

“You left without a word.”

“I know.”

“You didn’t call. Not once.”

“I know.”

Anne’s voice wavered — the first crack in her composure.

“And now you’re here because he took you back.”

Kai’s voice cut in, steady:

“Not took. I chose.”

That made Anne inhale sharply.

Marie reached for Anne’s hand under the table.  
 Alex looked away respectfully.  
 Nics stayed very, very still.

Anne blinked rapidly, throat tightening.

“Excuse me,” she whispered, standing.  
 “I need a minute.”

She walked away, not in anger — but like someone holding a cracked bowl with both hands, terrified of making it shatter.

The table exhaled collectively.

“That wasn’t… the worst,” Alex murmured.

“She didn’t throw anything,” Nics confirmed.

“She’s in pain,” Marie added.

Ileana nodded, voice thin. “I don’t blame her.”

Kai reached for her hand.  
 In front of everyone.  
 Deliberate.  
 Visible.

She took it without hesitation.

That — more than anything — shifted the air.

Marie smiled faintly.  
 Alex raised his glass.  
 Nics clinked hers against his.

They ate.  
 They talked.  
 Anne returned — eyes red, but present.  
 She sat.  
 She didn’t look at Ileana.  
 But she sat.

It was enough.

Dinner ended not with resolution, not with healing —  
 but with possibility.

A door cracked open.  
 Small.  
 Real.

The night air outside was cool, the city humming beneath it.

They walked to the hotel in near-silence, the tension of the dinner still clinging to their coats.

At the door of the room Kai had booked — two beds, warm lighting — Ileana exhaled.

“You okay?” he asked softly.

“No,” she whispered.  
 “But I’m not running.”

He nodded once.

“That’s enough.”

They brushed their teeth side by side.

He answered emails while she stood at the window, looking down at the city she had once abandoned.

Neither asked for closeness.  
 Neither stepped away, either.

When the lights finally dimmed, she murmured, “Kai?”

“Hm?”

“Thank you. For today.”

He didn’t say “you’re welcome.”  
 He said:

“We’re doing this right this time.”

She believed him.

A dangerous, hopeful thing.

Morning Flight

She fell asleep on his shoulder again.  
 He didn’t move.

When they landed, he drove them home through quiet streets.

Her voice trembled.

“Do they hate me?”

“No,” he said.  
 “They remember you.”

Sometimes that was worse.

Back Home — The Message

The moment they stepped inside, his phone buzzed.

Anne.  
 Three messages.

	 *I’m sorry I walked out.*

	 *I’m not okay.*

He showed her.  
 Didn’t hide anything.

Then the voice note played — Anne’s voice fragile and frightened:

“Kai… I’m trying to be okay with this. I am. But I’m scared. I’m scared you’ll break again. I’m scared she’ll leave again. I’m scared that losing her meant losing you—and now getting her back means losing you in a different way.

You’re so calm about it. I’m not. I’m terrified.

I don’t know how to exist if I lose you too.”

Ileana pressed her fingers to her lips.  
 “It’s my fault.”

“No,” Kai said softly. “It’s the truth. Truth hurts. But it can heal too.”

She closed her eyes.

“What do I do?”

“You show up,” he said.  
 “For her. For them. For us.”

She nodded.

He texted Anne:

*I’m here. I’m not vanishing. Neither is she.*  
 *We’re trying. We’re not running. I promise.*

He set the phone down.

Then:

“Come here.”

She sat beside him on the couch.  
 He wrapped an arm around her.  
 She melted — slowly, like thaw.

“Do you regret choosing me?” she whispered.

“No,” he said.  
 “I regret the years you were gone. Not this.”

She didn’t cry.  
 But her breathing changed — a quiet surrender.

In the Realm

The godling hovered in a corner of the citadel, watching the mortal pair collapse into a soft, aching sort of peace.

“She made him lighter,” it said.

“And heavier,” the entity replied.  
 “That is the cost of presence.”

“Is it bad?”

“No,” the entity murmured.  
 “It is simply human.”

Below, they held each other.  
 Not perfectly.  
 Not easily.  
 But willingly.

“A beginning,” the entity said.

The godling pulsed.  
 “Warm.”

# 

# **CHAPTER XXX** 

*The One-on-One* 

She came to the building without asking.

Ileana was making tea—half-heart and on autopilot, hands the steady things in the room—when Kai’s knock came at the door. He looked smaller in the doorway than she remembered; his hair a little longer at the ears, his face carrying months like quiet lines. He didn’t come in at first. He stood in the hall with a caution he reserved for glass and for other people’s grief.

“She’s downstairs,” he said, voice flat as news.

Ileana’s cup trembled in her fingers. “Anne?”

He nodded. “She came unannounced. She asked if I could talk to you.” He let the sentence hang in the seam of air that split the apartment in two.

She had rehearsed. In the micro-grammar of her worst nights she had run and recited apologies under her breath until they became dull instruments. She had written versions of this conversation in letters that had never been postmarked. Still, the thought of Anne at the bottom of his stair—an uninvited knock, an unexpected face—felt like a live wire.

“I’ll step out,” Kai said, before she could manage a response. “Call me when you’re ready. I’ll be right downstairs. I’ll bring something back.” He moved with the deliberate care of a man booking a small ceasefire.

She watched him go and the sound of his footsteps down the stairs made her breath catch. When the door clicked softly behind him the apartment felt too large and too empty at once.

A few minutes later the lock turned and Anne came in.

She was the same and not at all: hair a little looser than the last time, eyes rimmed with a tired defense, clothes chosen in the morning perhaps with the idea of armor. She glanced at Ileana with a look that carried three years’ worth of weather in it—scorch marks, rain stains, and that brittle, cautious bloom of someone who has learned how to hurt without breaking everything.

“I didn’t call,” Anne said, without preface. “I didn’t want you to rehearse how to be sorry.”

“I—” Ileana’s mouth closed around the apology. It had been practiced, polished, used as a shield. It felt meaningless now, a currency of small coins.

Anne sat at the edge of the couch as if measuring the give of the cushion. “Do you know what it felt like—” she began, voice taut, “—to be the one who stayed?”

“Yes,” Ileana said. The single syllable carried a weathered confession. “I know.”

Anne’s hands curled in her lap. “I held him while you… left. I took grocery runs, patched his shirts, read the letters he never sent. I put an extra plate on the table because I thought he’d come back to it. I listened to his brokenness and tried to stitch him into something tolerable. I cried for him at three in the morning while pretending to be okay at work. I kept him steady, Ileana. I thought that was love. I thought that—if I did that—if I was that—he would be safe.”

The words came in a rush and then she stopped, not wanting to warble the whole of her confession into static.

“I know,” Ileana said again, smaller this time. Her hands found the edge of her own sleeve and left damp marks.

Anne’s face tightened. “You left. You left me with the pieces and the blame. Do you understand that? Do you understand that every time someone asked about you I had to decide between anger and grief?” She laughed once, a sharp, disbelieving sound. “Do you know how much easier it would have been if you’d called? If you’d said, ‘I can’t—’ anything? Even that would have meant something.”

Because Ileana had none of those things to offer, she tried to make the only movement left that felt honest: she told the truth.

“I thought leaving was protection,” she said. “I thought leaving would stop me from ruining everything else. I left because I was tiny in myself. I left because panic felt like mercy. I left and I lied to myself that absence was kinder than presence. It wasn’t. It was cowardice dressed as kindness.”

Anne’s eyes went somewhere inside, watching, counting scars. She inhaled like someone cataloguing the shape of a wound. “You did more than leave,” she said quietly. “You erased us.”

“I know.” Her voice broke. “I know. I am sorry. I don’t have a rehearsal apology. I have… this. I’ve learned more by staying than I learned by leaving. I have read his letters. I have broken, truly. I am—” she stopped because words were small and insufficient in the face of what she had done.

Anne’s shoulders bunched. For a moment she looked like someone who might strike or flee. Instead she let her face bend inward as if listening to something only she could hear—her own old hurt, the slow clock of resentment.

“I am terrified,” Anne admitted, unexpectedly. The admission sounded like a crack in the armor. “I am terrified that you’ll make him whole and then the world will take him anyway and I won’t be able to fix him this time. I am terrified because I remember what it felt like to be only the shoulder while everything else left. I’m… jealous.” The word was a small stone rolled across the table.

Ileana’s mouth opened, then closed. The word lived in the space between them like a blunt instrument. For a long while they simply looked at each other: two friends who had once shared secrets like currency, now holding them like contraband.

“You were his anchor,” Ileana said, shame arriving like a tide. “I… I saw how you were there. I saw how you saved him in ways I never knew how to be. I could have asked for help instead of leaving. I could have said… anything. I’m sorry. For everything.”

Anne laughed again softly, a sound more haunted than amused. “Sorry. That word is simple, but it’s heavy, too. You say ‘sorry’ and it sits like gravel in your mouth and you still have to walk through the day.”

A silence thickened. Then Anne leaned forward and her voice changed from accusation to something rawer—need. “Do you know what I wanted, when you were gone? To come back with him. To say, ‘we can fix this.’ Instead he came back hollow. You were a story. He made you a story I could not touch.”

“That’s true,” Ileana whispered. “I was a story. I was afraid to be the messy part of his life.”

Anne’s eyes blinked, brimmed. “And now? Now you are here and everyone says—‘oh, give her a chance.’ But what about me? Who gives me a chance? Who tells me I’m allowed to be angry instead of grateful?”

Ileana swallowed. The question landed like a weight she had no right to carry but could not refuse.

“You’re allowed,” she said. The words came from a place that had learned how to be quiet and ethical through the steadiness of another person’s life. “You’re allowed to be angry. You’re allowed to be scared. You’re allowed to—” She hesitated, because the part of her that had been small all her life wanted to divide the world into people who deserved and people who did not. “You’re allowed to choose for yourself. I… I will not take that from you.”

Anne’s face crumpled, then steeled. She let out a laugh that was closer to a sob. “I don’t know if I can trust that.”

“Then don’t,” Ileana said, with a bluntness born of desperation. “I don’t expect blind faith. You expect proof. I want to give it. Start with small things. Let me show up at the things I used to flee. Let me show up when he’s sick. Let me—” She swallowed. “Let me make the coffee that he likes. Let me show you that I can do the work.”

Anne’s laugh this time was a whisper of incredulity. “You want to make him coffee? You used to make me coffee the wrong way.”

“I still do,” Ileana admitted, and for a brief, improbable second they both smiled—two friends remembering small absurdities from a life that had once been ordinary.

The room condensed into a thousand tiny memories—shared late-night secrets that became confessions, the way they’d once laughed about terrible exes, the time they’d painted Kai’s kitchen a color they both regretted. The archive of their friendship lay between them: a battered map of intimacy, accidents, and mutual salvation. It had been vandalized, yes; but it was still there.

Anne folded her hands and then opened them again. “There are nights I stayed up making sure he didn’t finally dissolve into the kind of person who only writes things and does nothing. I held things because I thought—if the world is chaotic, then someone needs to be the constant. I resented the constancy you gave him, paradoxically. I resented this person I loved more than I should have.” She exhaled, a long surrender. “And I’m scared you might become the reason he is broken again.”

Ileana swallowed. “I would rather break and try to fix than run away and leave the break behind like a secret.”

Anne shook her head, small and bitter. “You don’t get to pick the script now,” she said. “Not unilaterally. Not after what happened.”

“I know,” Ileana said. “I don’t expect to be handed forgiveness or instant trust. I want to do the smaller, uglier work.”

Anne’s eyes were raw and keen. She reached out then, a movement that looked like it might be a push or a pull—either would have been honest. Her fingers brushed Ileana’s wrist and for a moment there was electricity: the kind you get when you touch something you’ve both memorized and also fear.

“You made a choice,” Anne whispered. “To leave. You can say the reasons—fear, cowardice—but I still feel abandoned.”

“And I will carry that,” Ileana said. “If you want me to carry it as penance, I will. But I will not run.”

A long breath. The air was a taut wire.

Anne’s shoulders shook; then she turned her face, and the tears came like permission finally given. They were not theatrical—they were small, clean, and hot. She let one fall and then another. “I’m tired of being brave for all of us,” she sobbed, voice breaking. “I’m tired of patching people who vanish.”

Ileana’s body moved before her mind could catch up: she put both hands on Anne’s face, gentle, as if she were adjusting the tilt of a fragile instrument. “I’m sorry,” she said—this time without rehearsal. “For leaving you with the hurt. For making you carry things you never asked to carry.”

Anne let her hands stay there, then folded her face into Ileana’s palm like the surrender of someone coming ashore for the first time in a storm.

“I’m scared,” Anne admitted between the broken breaths. “But I don’t want to be the person who never tries.”

“Try,” Ileana said simply.

They sat with the small, tremulous thing that is the beginning of repair: a portal that asks two people to agree to small, regular truths. Anne’s head bowed into Ileana’s hands, and the room—Kai’s apartment that had recently learned how to breathe—held them both.

At some point there was a sound in the hallway: the door opening, a soft clink of steps, the careful tray against a palm.

Kai had come back not because he’d been summoned but because he had the habit of inhabiting both worlds when they needed a human hinge. He carried two cups of coffee and a small, unassuming sandwich for each of them—plain, kindly, exactly what they needed.

He paused in the doorway when he saw them, then set the tray down on the coffee table with the exacting fairness of someone who understood the arithmetic of grace.

“You okay?” he asked, voice careful.

Anne lifted her head. Her features were raw but there was a new sheen of something else—relief, perhaps, or permission. “We’re talking,” she said.

“Good,” he said, and there was no triumph in his tone. Only a deep, satisfied steadiness. He took a slow breath and then left them in the city of their conversation.

When he closed the door behind him the apartment felt smaller but steadier—two people had dismantled and then begun to rebuild something in a span of hours. The hum of the refrigerator and the distant traffic replaced the jagged edges of earlier words, and the world felt, for a while, like it made sense again.

Anne’s shoulders shuddered with a rough, almost laughable sound; then she wiped her face with the back of her hand and let out a single, weary breath. “I don’t know if I can trust you yet,” she said, voice small but steady. “But… I want to try.”

Ileana’s answer was immediate and bare: “I will wait. As long as you need.”

There was a small, comic absurdity in the domestic: the coffee tray, the sandwich, the quiet presence of the man who kept weathering their storms without apology. The way Kai’s apartment had learned to hold them—this was not a miracle. It was a room that had collected enough small kindnesses to be useful.

Anne let herself be held for a moment longer. Then she drew back slowly, eyes clearer.

“Start with small things,” she said, and a faint smile—tender, brittle—played at her mouth. “Show up. Make the appointments. Don’t leave without telling someone.”

“I will,” Ileana promised.

“And be honest when it hurts,” Anne added.

“I will.”

They made a list without paper: small, repeatable acts that could become architecture—coffee on bad mornings, a call when a shift runs late, being at the ward during a crisis, no disappearances for months, an attendance at birthdays, expressions of what they were actually feeling.

This was not redemption. It was not even a fragile truce. It was the first ledger of terms two damaged people could live with.

Anne stood then, more upright. “I’ll try,” she said. “But you should understand—this will reopen. It will cut. We’ll have to patch a hundred times.”

“Patch,” Ileana said. “We will patch. We will build seams into the thing we make together.”

They both laughed then, a wet, honest sound. It was not joy; it was the relief of hearing a human note after a long silence.

Anne made for the door. She hesitated, turning her face back once. “I’m not promising anything but I’m… starting.” Her voice was thinly brave.

Ileana watched her go and felt a small, searing gratitude. When the door clicked shut she closed her eyes and let out a breath that felt long overdue.

Kai returned to find them sitting quiet, the tray cleared except for the two half-eaten sandwiches. He sank into the armchair and watched them with an expression that was both relieved and wary—the way of someone who had lived with contraband hope for years and had just been told it might be legal again.

“It went… okay?” he asked.

“It went,” Anne’s voice came from the doorway, already softer, already human. “It went like a start.”

Kai nodded. He looked at Ileana, then at Anne, then at the small room that had borne witness to the conversation.

“Small repairs,” he said. “Daily ones. No miracles. No grand declarations.”

Anne looked at him—eyes wet, edges raw—and managed a small, crooked smile.

“Okay,” she said. “I’ll try.”

Later, when they lay down, when the apartment settled into its nocturnal hum, Ileana thought of the list they’d made without pen and paper. It felt heavy and precise and possible. Waiting was no longer a sentence but a shape—a line they could walk together.

The godling in the Citadel would later note the conversation as one of the small, human rituals that changed weather: two women patching an edifice of love with the only thing humans had that gods sometimes envied—grit. The entity would watch and, perhaps, finally allow itself a private, brittle optimism.

At the edge, in the soft after, Anne’s last voice had been the most vulnerable thing Ileana had heard in years: “I’m scared. But I don’t want to stop trying.”

“I will wait,” Ileana thought and said aloud to the dim room, to the sleeping city outside the window, and to the small creature of light that had learned her name upstairs.

“I will wait as long as you need.”

# **CHAPTER XXXI** 

*The Godling Notices*

She dreamed of light first.

It arrived like the shape of a child's curiosity — not full-bodied, not entirely formed, but brilliant enough to hurt if she looked at it too long. It hovered above her pillow, a small, star-born thing with no edges, only an eager, pulsing center. It smelled faintly like bread just taken from an oven and the deep, indistinct scent of his letters: paper, coffee, a trace of his cologne. The light tilted in the air the way a bird tilts its head when interested.

“Hello,” it said, but not with ordinary words. The voice came folded into images.

He warms around you.

You smell like returning.

The grammar was wrong — childlike and mythic at once — and it made her chest loosen as if someone had finally unknotted a string tied tight at her sternum. In the dream the godling circled her like a comet trying to understand the shape of a planet; it left a trail of tiny constellations that glittered where it passed.

She did not wake afraid. She woke with the taste of pastry and ink on her tongue and a small, ridiculous sense that someone had come to see the way she breathed. She lay still for a long minute, hand on the sheet, the dark of the apartment folding around her like a question.

Kai was awake before she was. He sat on the couch with his shoes off, fingers steepled, a book forgotten open on his knee. When he saw the way she had curled into herself the light in the room seemed to bend reflexively toward them, small and private.

“You dream?” he asked.

She told him about the star-child without embellishment, the way one reports something that must be true because the body insists it was.

“It said things,” she said. “It said—” and then the phrases came back like a child reciting a poem. “He warms around you. You smell like returning.”

He listened with a quiet so attentive it felt like a hand laid over her small, raw places. When she finished he didn’t make jokes. He didn’t try to rationalize it into safety. He let the sentence be what it had been—strange and tender.

“Go for a walk with me?” he asked suddenly.

She blinked, surprised. The afternoon pressed at the windows like a warm, tentative thing. She wandered for a second, still in the wet aftermath of a dream, and then tucked on her shoes without asking why. He slipped his coat on as if he had been waiting for permission.

They walked out into the city and the ordinary world—traffic, a dog that barked at a pigeon, a vendor folding up his stall—watched them in their small ritual. Kai held a coffee in one hand and walked close enough that their shoulders brushed without pretense. The air felt like a sheet between them: not a barrier, only an agreed canvas.

“What did it look like?” he asked after a few blocks, voice low.

“A small light,” she said. “Curious. It said things I couldn’t parse straight away. It called me… it called me with a voice that wasn’t language.”

He nodded as if this made sense in a way no words could hold. They walked through a small park where the trees were not yet full with young green; their shadows made cool lanes on the pavement. Kai’s thumb found the seam of her hand and rubbed once, a private punctuation.orbit

He began then, careful as someone handing over a fragile object.

“The godling notices everything it can,” he said. “It is young. It is not trying to be holy—just… hungry for patterns. For warmth. For the way things stick together.”

She listened to him with the same attention she had given surgeons’ instructions, the discipline of a person who needed data to be allowed to feel.

“It called me ‘the one he loves’?” she asked, echoing something she had not meant to say aloud.

“It tried,” Kai said. “It doesn’t have our grammar. It maps by the things that change weather.” He searched for the way to say what mattered without breaking the ordinary skin of the world. “It will call you many names, I suspect. It has a list already.” He smiled — small, proud, embarrassed. “It calls people in metaphors. It is terrible at nouns but brilliant at weather.”

She laughed, an absurd, wet sound. “Is that an official title now?”

“Only if you accept it,” he said. “Which you are allowed to do or not.”

They paused beside a small fountain and the city’s sound hunched into a soft surround. A pigeon unwrapped some bread with obsessive focus. A child ran past, trailed by a parent’s patience. The godling’s language ran through her head again, the metaphors almost clinical in their honesty.

“You are the one he loves.” It had meant, clumsily, that she had become a point of gravity in his orbit. The words landed on her like a mapmaker’s pencil: precise and impossible to ignore.

“Why would it pick me?” she asked finally, because the question was more complicated than gratitude or vanity. “Why me and not another friend? Another person who stayed?”

Kai didn’t answer right away. He folded his hands in his coat pockets, looked down at their touching shoulders, and then back up, watching the way the light had bent across her cheek.

“Because the realm responds,” he said. He chose his words like a man measuring the length of a table before setting a plate. “The realm responds to who matters.”

She waited for the weight of the sentence to fall. It came like a stone thrown into a lake: round and inevitable.

“That sounds symbolic,” she said, trying its edges. “Like a mythic way to say I’m important to him.”

“It’s not symbolic,” he said, and there it was — a tremor in his tone that made the air between them listen. “It’s reflex.”

He inhaled and then said more, softer, reverent.

“When he cares, the Citadel rearranges itself. It’s not performance. It’s… physics for the divine. The godling notices warmth and it learns what to orbit. It notices anchors. If someone becomes an anchor for him—real, steady, honest—then the architecture answers to that. It makes space. It clears windows. It makes room for that warmth the same way a tide makes room for a boat.”

She heard the reverence in his voice and felt something shift under the ribs that had been braced for a long time. To be a part of what steadied his divinity was more than flattery; it was a responsibility that felt like a small, bright burden.

“That’s… a lot,” she said, steadier than she felt.

“It’s everything,” he allowed. “And it’s nothing like a throne. It’s the opposite of domination. It’s the shape care takes in a realm that already knows paradox. The realm doesn’t bow. It houses. It refuses to make shrines. It simply opens closets.”

He searched her face, seeing her process, trying to map where this sat on the scale between terror and belonging.

“You can be scared,” he said, more plea than permission. “You can run. And I will still be here in the middle, not angry. But I’d like you to know the facts so you can choose.”

She felt again the soft, ridiculous gratitude of a person who had been given the right to be complicated. She recalled the dream, the child-like light that had greeted her like a guest at a table.

“What did it call me?” she asked, a small child wanting the name of a new toy.

He recited the godling’s awkward metaphors the way one repeats wiring for a house: careful, exact.

“You are the missing constellation,” he said. “A mother seed. The warm axis around which I stabilize. You make the paradox feel less violent. You make me warm.”

She let each phrase slide through her like water. They were not songs, not prayers—merely observations the godling made in the crude human-language it was learning.

“You smell like home-coming,” he added, repeating another of its metaphors, the one that had come in the dream like the scent of bread. He both meant it for her and for the way the godling had translated the thing into its small vocabulary.

She closed her eyes for one slow blink and then another.

“Do you think it loves me,” she asked, the question small and humiliating and enormous.

He took her hand like an oath. “It loves what you let it see. It loves what you let us become. It’s not the same as human love. It’s a kind of recognition. But recognition is not small.”

She nodded. Recognition sounded like permission. Permission felt like progress.

They walked on, and the city opened up its sidewalks like a practiced audience. The godling had not followed them in form; it was better at the liminal work — the soft changes that happen without fanfare. Yet she felt its attention like static across her skin. At odd moments she glanced up and felt the thin, curious pressure of a presence: a warm node behind the leaves, a glint that washed over a passerby and found her again. Each time she relaxed a fraction more.

“Will it always be like this?” she asked.

“In time,” he said. “The godling will learn more words. It will make metaphors out of things that feel silly to us. It will be clumsy. But it will protect what keeps its attention steady. And that reflex — that room-making that happens when someone matters — will become part of how it maps the world.”

She tried to imagine a world that rearranged itself for love as dutifully as the sea rearranges itself for the tide. The image was both absurd and luminous.

“Are you ashamed?” she asked abruptly. “To have something like that—some realm that answers you?”

He stopped, an almost invisible hesitation. The question grazed an old wound: the worry that his strangeness might be a bridge she could not cross.

“Sometimes,” he admitted. “Once, I thought it made me dangerous to love. I thought it would swallow people whole with its logic. But you’ve shown me something different. You make the thing kinder. You ask it to be smaller in the ways that matter.”

She warmed at that, small and private, like someone given a compliment they’d been too afraid to hope for.

They sat for a while on a bench under a tree that dragged the sun into ribs of gold. The godling’s presence softened around them like a sheaf of light; no grand revelation flared. This was incremental: a dream, a walk, a soft phrase spoken in the language of someone who had learned to live in the weight of paradox.

When they returned to the apartment the godling was already at the window, a small bright smear that retreated into the folds of glass when it noticed them. It pulsed once—fast, as if delighted—and then folded itself into the corner like a cat curling tight.

Kai closed the curtains with a deliberate, theatrical slowness. Inside the room, the two of them sat on the couch and let the knowledge settle like a new, useful fact.

“Thank you for staying,” he said, almost whispering. The trembling in his voice was not fear so much as gratitude and an ache he no longer had to edit away.

She leaned into him, head against his shoulder, the proximity like a practiced prayer.

“You’re welcome,” she said. And because the claim wanted more weight she added, “I don’t fully understand it yet. But I trust you.”

He kissed the crown of her head, reverent. “You don’t have to understand everything. You can let it happen slowly. That is the whole point.”

The godling chirped from the corner — an infant’s laugh translated into light. It had learned one new phrase since the dream.

“You are the one he loves,” it pulsed.  
 “You are soft gravity,” it hummed.  
 “You are the place he rests.”

It meant them all in its awkward, true language.

Kai wrapped himself around her like an arm of careful harbor. Outside, the city made its slow, human noises. Inside, the apartment settled into a peace that was not simplistic but honest — the sort that comes when myth and day-to-day life braid together without violence.

She slept that night with a small, steady smile. The godling visited her in dreams again, lighter this time, playful, leaving constellations in the seams of the sheets. Each visit taught her less about gods and more about being permitted to be a center for someone’s care.

The realm had noticed. It responded. And being noticed did not unmake her; it only allowed her to be one more honest thing in the geometry of his life.

When he spoke again, before sleep took them both, his voice trembled with reverence and a hint of fear, the two threading together like honest beads.

“The realm responds to who matters,” he said, voice small, luminous.  
 “It’s reflex,” he added, reverent and shaking. “It bends toward what keeps me from fracturing.”

She tightened her hand in his and let the sentence sit like a blossom in her palm. The ending of the chapter felt like the hush after a bell: peace and a kind of hopeful gravity, as if the universe had learned a new rule and the two of them were, finally, allowed to be normal inside it.

# **CHAPTER XXXII**

 *The Hope Calls Her Mom*

It happened on a Tuesday.

She had just finished her hospital shift and was standing in the hallway outside Kai’s unit—tired, damp with humidity, hair falling out of its tie—when she felt something warm press against her ankle.

She looked down.

A notebook was leaning upright against her leg.

Not an ordinary notebook.  
 Her name was pressed in gold on the front—*Ileana*—in handwriting that was definitely not Kai’s.

The notebook wiggled.  
 Very gently.

“Oh my God,” she whispered, more breath than voice. “Not this again.”

The notebook fluttered open like a bird opening a wing.  
 A single page flipped, then another, and tiny motes of light escaped the edges of the paper like dust made of stars.

Then a small voice—broken, bright, childlike—hummed:

“Mom.”

She froze.

“Mom,” the notebook said again.  
 Then, with more certainty,

“Mom. First warmth. Origin.”

“Oh—oh no—Kai?” she croaked, backing up against his door. “KAIIII?”

The notebook hopped once.

“Origin.”

She slapped the door in panic. The lock clicked and the door swung open so fast she stumbled inside and almost fell on top of him.

Kai caught her by the elbows.  
 “Are you okay? What happened? Why are you—”

The notebook skittered into the room behind her, glowing like a smug star.

Kai looked at it.  
 Then at her.  
 Then at it again.

He sighed.  
 A very long, resigned sigh.

“…It learned the trick,” he muttered.

“The WHAT?\!” she shrieked.

He rubbed his face. “The entity—the Notebook Act, you remember? Of course you do. Apparently—”

He stopped himself.

Because the notebook had begun to melt—light dripping off the pages like warmth made liquid—rising into the form of that small godling she had dreamed of, now hovering inches above the floor.

The godling blinked its soft, luminous eyes at Ileana, then beamed like a child recognizing a parent for the first time.

“Mom\!”

She stared at it helplessly.  
 “Kai. Why is it calling me Mom? I didn’t— I didn’t do anything—”

Kai raised both hands, trying to soothe both of them.  
 “It doesn’t know what ‘mom’ means. For godlings it’s just a placeholder. A word for ‘first warmth’ or ‘stabilizing presence’ or—”

The godling spun sharply, glaring at him—or the divine-light equivalent of glaring.

“No.”

Kai blinked.  
 “What?”

“I know what ‘mom’ means.”  
 The godling crossed its tiny arms of light.  
 “She’s half the reason I exist.”

Ileana choked on air.  
 Kai made a noise somewhere between horror and awe.

The godling pointed at Kai.  
 “You are Dad.”

Kai froze.  
 “Pardon?” he whispered.

“Dad,” the godling repeated confidently.  
 “You made me, but she made me *feel*.”

It floated closer to Ileana.  
 Soft. Sure.

“She is Mom.”

“I—I didn’t—” Ileana sputtered.

“You *did*,” the godling said serenely. “You anchored him. You softened him. When he grew, I grew. So half of me is him—”  
 She tapped her tiny chest of light.  
 “—and half is you.”

Kai made an inhuman sound—halfway to a swear, halfway to a prayer.

The godling drifted closer, hovering by Ileana’s knee. Then, with the innocent chaos of a toddler, she tried to climb into Ileana’s bag, poked at her ID badge, and then rolled herself into a ball of light that nudged Ileana’s water bottle as if checking if it belonged to *her*. The bottle tipped slightly and righted itself, as if accommodating her.

“Why is she inspecting my hydration choices?” Ileana whispered.

Kai deadpanned, “She’s domesticating you.”

Before Ileana could gather a reply, the godling floated into her hands like a warm star curling into a lap.

“You don’t have a name yet?” Ileana whispered, voice trembling.

The godling shook her head and leaned into Ileana’s palm.

“Then…” Ileana swallowed hard, heart pounding. “I’ll give you one.”

Kai stared at her, breath held.

Ileana took a long, quiet moment to look at the small creature in her hands.  
 Then, softly, gently, reverently:

“Hope.”

The godling lit up like a sunrise.

“Hope,” she echoed, voice shimmering.  
 *“Hopehopehope— I’m Hope\!”*

The entire room warmed.  
 A pulse went through the Citadel—far above, far away—as if reality itself bowed to the naming.

Kai whispered, shaken, “You named her.”

Hope pressed her forehead to Ileana’s.  
 “Mom named me. I am Hope.”

Then—domestic chaos:  
 Hope immediately zipped to the kettle and chirped at it like greeting a metallic sibling. The kettle clicked on by itself.  
 Hope zipped to the plant on the shelf, circled it twice, and made it bloom an inch taller.

“Hope,” Ileana gasped, horrified. “No enhancing the houseplants without consent\!”

Hope dimmed sheepishly.  
 “Learning.”

Lesser echoes materialized at the baseboards, peeking out shyly.  
 The air seemed to thicken with warmth.  
 The realm bent around her gently—reflexively—as though accepting a new gravitational law.

Then—

A crack of displaced air.

The entity appeared.  
 Flustered.  
 Half-formed.  
 Visibly panicking.

“What—WHAT DID YOU—WHY IS SHE—HOW—”  
 Its hands flew to its head.  
 “WHAT KIND OF GOD LETS A GODLING CALL A HUMAN ‘MOM’?\!\!”

Hope floated smugly.  
 “Because she is.”

The entity whirled toward Kai.  
 “YOU LET A HUMAN INTO THE DIVINE LINEAGE?\!”

Kai shrugged helplessly.  
 “Paradox did it.”

The entity groaned into its palms.  
 “We are doomed. Absolutely doomed.”

Hope drifted up to it.  
 “You can be Uncle.”

The entity stared at her.

Kai bit his lip to stop laughing.  
 Ileana hid her face in her hands.

“…Fine,” the entity whispered, resigned. “I suppose a human in the divine realm won’t kill anyone *immediately*.”

Hope chirped happily and snuggled into Ileana’s shoulder.

Then, in the most domestic absurdity imaginable, Hope floated over to Kai’s mug, inspected it, sniffed the steam, and declared:

“Dad drinks sadness-flavored tea.”

Kai spluttered.  
 Ileana wheezed.  
 The Entity sighed like a disappointed elder.

And just like that—  
 with a name, a bond, and one chaotic divine uncle—  
 Ileana became part of the mythic ecosystem.

# **INTERLUDE IV**

*The Entity Files a Complaint*

The Citadel did not like surprises.

It tolerated paradox, endured recursion, and had once survived a century-long civil war between two adjectives. But surprises—true surprises—made the walls contract like irritated lungs.

So when Hope, freshly named, freshly smug, and freshly attached to a human, flared into the realm for the first time…

Everything screamed.

Silently.  
 Politely.  
 But unmistakably.

A ripple went through the marble logic of the halls. The glyphs on the ceiling inverted themselves in distress. The god-architecture shifted as if whispering to itself, *She should not be here, she should not be here, she should not be here—*

Then Kai stepped through.

And the Citadel stopped screaming.  
 Just… hesitated.

The Entity did not hesitate.

It appeared in front of him mid-stride, hair fracturing into geometric shards with sheer indignation.

“Kai,” it said, with the brittle calm of someone who has just watched a toddler set the sun on fire. “Explain.”

Hope peeked from behind Kai’s coat like a child hiding from a teacher.  
 “Hi Uncle,” she chirped.

The Entity pinched the bridge of its non-existent nose.

“No. No. Absolutely not. Do you have any comprehension—any at *all*—of what it means that the godling manifested in the mortal plane?” It pointed at Kai as though poking a hole in logic itself. “She is a conceptual creature\! A living paradox\! She should destabilize the entire human layer\!”

Kai shrugged. “She was fine.”

“*She was fine?* She called a human ‘Mom,’ Kai\!”

“That part was surprising,” Kai admitted.

The Entity dragged both hands down its face.  
 “You cannot simply allow divine constructs to imprint on humans. Humans are porous. Humans are—”

“Important,” Kai said.

The Entity froze.

“…What?”

“Important,” Kai repeated softly. “She matters. And Hope understands that.”

Hope beamed, flaring just enough to make the nearest column flicker with parental pride.

The Entity stared at her, then at Kai, then at the wider Citadel as if begging the architecture for help.

The architecture stayed neutral.  
 Indifferent.  
 Maddeningly unhelpful.

“Kai,” it said eventually, “this is ontologically reckless. If the godling exists fully in the mortal plane, she will start rewriting physics by accident. Mortals will start seeing things that should not be seen. *Reality* will misfile itself.”

Hope lifted her hand.  
 “I only bloomed a plant,” she said defensively.

“It was not a plant,” the Entity hissed. “It was an apartment fern with identity issues. It has now begun composing poetry.”

A pause.

“Is it good poetry?” Kai asked.

“KAI.”

He lifted both palms in surrender.  
 “Okay. I get it. She’s dangerous down there.”

Hope looked stricken.  
 “I don’t mean to be.”

“And that,” the Entity muttered, “is precisely the problem.”

Kai exhaled—deep, slow, deliberate.  
 And the realm quieted.

The Entity stepped back, wary.  
 “…What are you doing.”

“Thinking.”

“Oh no.”

Hope floated closer to him, clinging to the sleeve of his coat like a child preparing for bad news.

Then Kai said the sentence that doomed everyone:

“I’ll keep them both here.”

The Entity jolted.  
 “*What?*”

“Here,” Kai repeated. “In the realm. With me. If her presence in the mortal plane is a threat, then she shouldn’t go there anymore.”

The Entity stared, horrified.  
 “You want to… move her in?”

“No,” Kai corrected. “I want her to be allowed in. Safely. Sometimes. When she wants to. Under rules.”

“Oh good,” the Entity said flatly. “Rules. Yes, rules always work when dealing with cosmic recursion, infant gods, and love.”

Kai ignored the jab.  
 He continued:

“She’s paradox-adjacent now. Naming Hope tied her to the realm anyway. If she steps inside, she’ll stabilize. The architecture will adapt around her instead of resisting her.”

The Entity blinked, processing.

“…You mean to contain her paradox within your domain.”

“Yes.”

“That is—”  
 The Entity stopped.  
 Attempted to form a sentence.  
 Failed.

Hope spoke instead.  
 “Mom can visit?”

“Only if she wants to,” Kai said. “Only when it’s safe.”

The Entity made a strangled sound.  
 “You cannot simply combine divine containment theory with human visitation schedules. That isn’t how metaphysics works\!”

“It’s how mine works,” Kai said quietly.

And the Citadel—traitorous, sentimental, unjustifiably fond of him—shimmered in agreement.

Hope clapped her tiny light-hands.  
 “Family realm\!”

“NO,” the Entity barked. “Not ‘family realm.’ This is not a daycare.”

Hope floated upside-down in delight.  
 “Family realm.”

Kai cleared his throat.  
 “So. Ground rules?”

The Entity stared at him as though witnessing the birth of a new, stupid universe.

But it relented.

Because it always relented with him.

“…Fine,” it said, defeated. “Rules.”

Kai nodded.  
 Hope spun like a happy comet.  
 The Citadel hummed, adjusting its geometry in real time.

The Entity lifted one glowing finger.

“Rule One: She may only enter the realm through *you*.”

“Agreed,” Kai said.

“Rule Two: She does not touch any structural metaphors. They are delicate.”

Hope whispered loudly, “I will touch ALL the metaphors.”

“NO YOU WILL NOT.”

Kai hid a smile.

“Rule Three,” the Entity continued, flustered, “She cannot—under any circumstances—give names to any more divine constructs.”

Hope wilted.  
 “Please?”

“ABSOLUTELY NOT.”

Kai reached down and ruffled the godling’s light gently.  
 “She already named one. That’s enough.”

Hope glowed, smug again.

“And Rule Four,” the Entity said, voice dropping to something almost human, almost sad, “she must understand that the realm is not a toy.”

“I’ll make sure she does,” Kai said.

A long, weighted silence settled over them.

The Entity’s shoulders lowered.

“…You really care for her,” it said, softer than before.

Kai did not answer.  
 He didn’t need to.

Hope answered for him, hopping onto his shoulder and curling there like a star settling into orbit.

“Dad warms around her,” she announced proudly.

The Entity closed its eyes in resignation.

“Very well,” it said. “Let the human inside.”

\*\*

And somewhere in the mortal world—  
 washing her hands, unaware of cosmic negotiations—  
 Ileana felt a brief shimmer across her spine.

Like something had just made room for her  
 in a place she had never stepped foot in.

Yet.

# **CHAPTER XXXIII**

*The First Visit*

It happened on a Friday morning.

No dream.  
 No feverish liminality.  
 No unconscious drifting.

She was awake.

And she was terrified.

Kai stood by her doorway, hands in his pockets, shoulders sloped in the careful posture of someone offering a world rather than imposing it.

“You don’t have to,” he said again.

“I know,” she replied. “That’s why I’m saying yes.”

Her voice was steady.  
 Her heartbeat was not.

Hope emerged from behind Kai’s shoulder like a small lantern climbing out of a coat pocket.

“Mom visits\!” the godling sang, bright with a reverence that made her cheeks burn.

“Not ‘Mom visits,’” Ileana said weakly. “Just… visiting.”

Hope touched her forehead with tiny light-fingers.  
 “Yes,” she agreed solemnly. “Visiting. Mom.”

Kai coughed into his fist, pretending not to laugh.

He reached for her hand—hesitant, offering—and she took it.

The air around them thinned, as if the world were inhales away from becoming something else.

“Just step with me,” Kai murmured. “Nothing dramatic.”

She stepped.

And the mortal world folded.

It was not light.  
 It was not darkness.

It was **architecture rearranging itself to become polite**.

The Citadel materialized around her in a rippling tilt—halls forming like thoughts, columns emerging like bones, air woven from a memory the realm had been practicing for weeks.

The ground beneath her feet felt like stone taught to imitate softness.

Her first breath was surprisingly easy.

Her second tasted faintly like his letters.

Her third—

—made the entire Citadel exhale.

Kai felt it too.  
 He tightened his grip on her hand.

“You good?” he asked softly.

“I… think?” she whispered. “I don’t feel like I’m dying.”

“That’s the highest praise the divine realm has ever received,” Kai muttered.

Then Hope burst into full brightness, circling Ileana like a comet welcoming a long-lost star.

“Mom is REAL here\!” she exclaimed.

“Please,” Ileana said, trying not to panic, “please keep expectations realistic—”

A shadow stretched across the hallway.

The Entity arrived.

The Entity had clearly prepared a speech.

It had not prepared *her*.

The moment Ileana stepped fully into the Citadel’s geometry, something in the Entity’s expression—normally a perfect blend of ancient annoyance and cosmic restraint—**glitched**.

Not metaphorically.

Literally.

The lines of its form hiccuped, realigning twice before settling.

“You’re—”  
 It cleared its throat.  
 “You are here.”

“Hi,” Ileana said politely, because what else did a human say to a divine bureaucrat having an existential crisis.

The Entity blinked so slowly it almost rebooted.

Hope floated proudly.  
 “Mom contains paradox safely.”

“She is not your—\!”  
 The Entity cut itself off, looked at Kai as if this was his fault, then turned back to Ileana with a strained smile.

“…Welcome to the Citadel.”

The welcome stressed several structural metaphors.

A column cracked somewhere behind them.

The Entity coughed.  
 “Don’t worry about that. It does that. Sometimes.”

Ileana glanced at Kai.

“Is it supposed to crack?”

“It’s being dramatic,” Kai whispered.

The Entity narrowed its eyes.

*The First Hour: A Family, If You Tilt Your Head*

The Citadel adapted around her in real time.

Not dramatically.  
 Not worshipfully.

More like a house making room for someone it wasn’t sure was staying the night.

The walls softened their angles.

Light dimmed to a wavelength her eyes recognized.

Temperature stabilized around “slightly colder than Kai’s hand.”

Hallways shortened instinctively, refusing to overwhelm.

Hope perched on her shoulder like a star-shaped parrot.

Kai walked beside her, close enough to anchor without crowding.

The Entity followed two steps behind, like a reluctant chaperone at a school field trip.

They showed her:

The library where metaphors congregated like birds

The balcony where the Citadel watched storms it created itself  
The Hall of Quiet Accords, which tried to bow but Kai waved it off

Hope’s tiny corner, full of light-scribbles and half-formed constellations

Ileana paused, touched the edge of a floating glyph.

It leaned into her palm.

Warm.

Alive.

And for one terrifying second—  
 —the entire realm brightened.

Kai stepped forward instantly.  
 His hand brushed her back.

“Easy,” he whispered. “It’s just reacting to you.”

The Entity hissed, “The architecture is impressionable. Please do not bond with the furniture.”

Meanwhile, in the Mortal Plane…

Her absence left a mark.

Not loud.

But noticeable.

Subtle ontological phenomena began appearing around the hospital:

A dying plant revived overnight

A fluorescent light stopped flickering after years of tantrums

A hallway avoided being crowded at shift change, as if time bent to let her walk through

A nurse commented, “It smelled like warm air today. No reason. Just… nice.”

Reality didn’t shudder.

It **adjusted**.

Small.  
 Instinctive.  
 As though her presence in the Citadel tugged gently on the mortal realm’s loose threads.

After an hour, the Citadel dimmed—not in warning, but in a way that felt like fatigue.

The Entity cleared its throat.

“The realm will tolerate her more easily over time,” it said. “But for today… this is enough.”

Hope clung to Ileana’s arm.

“Mom come back?” she asked softly.

Ileana swallowed.

“Yeah,” she whispered. “I’ll come back.”

Hope flared so brightly the nearest wall began forming celebratory patterns.  
 The Entity snapped its fingers, mortified, and the wall stopped.

Kai stepped closer.

“Ready?” he murmured.

She nodded.

He took her hand.

The Citadel folded.

And the mortal world returned around her like a breath she hadn’t noticed she was holding.

Her apartment reassembled around her: the soft hum of the AC, the faint smell of detergent, the mug on the table exactly where she left it. But the room felt subtly retextured — like the light had learned her outline and now hung differently around her.

Kai sat beside her on the couch, elbows on his knees, head slightly bowed as if bracing for her verdict.  
 Hope perched on the backrest like a glowing parrot pretending to blend in with the décor.

Ileana let out a shaky breath.

“…It didn’t feel wrong,” she said.

Kai turned toward her, something loosening in his shoulders.  
 “It won’t ever feel wrong,” he murmured. “Just new.”

Hope’s tiny voice chimed: “Mom is now multi-realm compatible\!”

The Entity’s disembodied voice groaned into existence from nowhere.  
 “PLEASE stop calling her that.”

“You are Uncle,” Hope replied, serene.

“I AM NOT—”  
 The voice cut itself off with a cosmic sigh.  
 “…fine. Whatever.”

Kai smirked. Ileana laughed despite herself.

Silence settled, warm and trembling and impossibly gentle.

Ileana tucked her knees up, turning to face him fully.  
 “So…” she said slowly, “we’re a family now, huh?”

Kai opened his mouth.  
 Closed it.  
 Gestured vaguely at Hope, at the ceiling, at the air.  
 “Well— I mean— family is a big word—”

“Hope literally calls you Dad.”

Kai winced. “…She sure does.”

“And the Entity is apparently our cranky uncle.”

“Regrettably.”

“And I just got welcomed to a divine realm like it was orientation day.”

Kai rubbed the back of his neck, eyes soft with that specific brand of helpless devotion he only had for her. “Look, if this is too much—”

“It’s not.”  
 She said it before she even thought about softening it.  
 “It’s… actually kind of nice.”

He blinked. “Nice?”

She looked down at her hands, flexing her fingers slowly.  
 “It’s nice,” she repeated quietly, “knowing that there are… gods. Real ones. Looking after me.”  
 Her throat bobbed.  
 “And that one of them loves me.”

Kai froze.

A beat.

Then—

“That’s not— I’m not— I’m not a god.”

“You have a realm,” she countered.
