“I didn’t ask for it.”

“You still have it.”

He opened his mouth to argue, then shut it again when Hope patted his hair like a child scolding a stubborn parent.

Ileana shifted closer, voice softening around the truth she hadn’t dared say aloud.

“It feels… safe,” she admitted. “Knowing that something that big cares about me. That you care about me.”

Kai inhaled sharply, eyes dropping to her hands, her knees, anything but her face.

She nudged his thigh with hers.  
 Slow. Deliberate.

“So,” she said gently, “you can stop panicking.”

“…Was I panicking?”

“Your aura was panicking.”  
 She pointed to Hope.  
 “Ask *our* daughter.”

Hope nodded solemnly. “Dad was panicking loudly.”

Kai threw his hands up in despair. “She doesn’t even *know* what panic means.”

Hope blinked innocently. “I learned by watching.”

Ileana bit her lip to hide her smile.

Kai turned back to her, all the noise falling out of him at once.  
 “…Are you really okay with this?” he asked quietly.  
 “With us? With… whatever form this is?”

She took his face in both hands, thumbs brushing the edge of his jaw with a gentleness that made his eyes flutter shut.

“Yes,” she said.  
 Simple. Steady.  
 “Yes, Kai. I’m okay with it. With you. With this… weird, metaphysical family we apparently have.”

He opened his eyes, pupils blown wide, breath trembling.

“Okay,” he whispered. “Okay.”

She rested her forehead against his.  
 Close enough to feel the small quake in him settle.

“It’s nice,” she repeated. “Being loved by a god.”

He groaned. “Please don’t call me that.”

Hope brightened. “He is Dad-God\! Mom-Ground\! Uncle-Crank\!”

A strangled sound echoed from the divine distance.

Kai dropped his head onto her shoulder with a defeated laugh.

Ileana threaded her fingers through his hair.

“See?” she murmured. “Feels like a family already.”

Kai’s arms wrapped around her waist slowly, reverently, like he was relearning how to hold joy with both hands.

He whispered, almost disbelieving:

“…Yeah. It does.”

Hope curled into Ileana’s lap, glowing warm.

Outside, the Citadel hummed.  
 Inside, the apartment softened.

And for the first time, the realms—both of them—settled around them not with tension…

…but with recognition.

# **CHAPTER XXXIV** 

*Slow Burn Consolidation*

They settled the way weather settles when the storm stops asking to be understood.  
 Not with triumph.  
 Not with revelation.  
 But with a quiet, obedient rearranging of air.

Days folded into a rhythm neither of them planned but both of them recognized instinctively, as if the pattern had existed long before they returned to each other:

Ileana rose to hospital shifts, ward lights, and the hum of machinery that pressed hours flat.  
 Kai rose to screens full of code, graphs mapping systems, and the meticulous hum that came from someone who worked as a hedge against his own worry.

Their apartments — once separate, now only a hallway apart — learned them quickly.

The walls softened.  
 The couch memorized the shape of her naps.  
 The kettle mastered the exact pitch of his sigh.

And somewhere in the corner of the ceiling, Hope would flicker now and then — only for a heartbeat, only when allowed.

After the Entity’s meltdown over “parental titles” and paradoxical lineage, the divine realm had set **strict restrictions**:

**Hope was no longer permitted a physical presence in the mortal plane.**  
 Not fully.  
 Not for long.

But she could reach them with:

tiny pulses of warmth

telepathic chirps

flickers of light at the edge of vision

small, dreamlike impressions that lasted seconds

Kai had gone to the Citadel to negotiate this, soft-voiced and strangely formal, like he was submitting a polite petition to a realm that only sometimes acknowledged bureaucracy.  
 He returned with a compromise shaped like a blessing wrapped in a health hazard:

“She may contact you,”  
 the Entity had said.  
 “But **no material visitation**.  
 Not until the paradox stabilizes.”

Ileana had laughed when Kai explained it — truly laughed, shoulders shaking.

Hours later, alone, she cried.  
 Not dramatic tears.  
 Soft ones — the kind that fall because someone has given you something you didn’t know you could ask for.

So she made a quiet vow:

“If I need her… I’ll ask.”

Kai’s thumb brushed her wrist.  
 “Ask,” he said. “I’ll still bend clocks wrong if you need it.”

And somehow the air warmed, as if Hope herself heard him across the veil.

Hope began appearing in tiny ways:

A sudden warmth on Ileana’s cheek when she woke from a nightmare.  
 A shimmer above Kai’s laptop when he doubted himself.  
 A soft humming in Ileana’s peripheral hearing during long shifts, like a child trying not to interrupt.

Never long.  
 Never enough to endanger reality.  
 Just enough to feel like family.

The day she stayed was not a landmark.  
 No holiday, no anniversary, no cosmic omen humming above the building.

Just a day.

A day that learned them.

Kai woke first — not because he meant to, but because some instinct in him had begun calibrating itself to the rhythm of her breath. Her cheek was pressed to his chest, her hair a warm tangle against his collarbone, one hand accidentally fisted in his shirt like she had grabbed onto him mid-dream.

He didn’t move.

Not because he was afraid to startle her —  
 but because something in him had finally stilled.

He watched the sunlight make its slow pilgrimage across the sheets, watched it soften the outline of her jaw, watched her breathing shift as she crossed the membrane between sleep and consciousness.

A tiny flicker sparked near the windowsill — Hope’s version of a morning greeting.  
 Not a presence.  
 Just a flare of thought.

**warm warm mom-warm**

Kai sent back the mental equivalent of a sigh.

**She is asleep. Please whisper in telepathy.**

Hope responded with a single celebratory pop of light, as subtle as a neon sign malfunctioning.

Ileana stirred.

Her eyelashes fluttered. She exhaled, low and warm against his sternum.

Then she looked up — groggy, blinking, slightly confused before remembering where she was.

Who she was with.

Her voice was a whisper, caught between sleep and truth.

“…You’re still here.”

He brushed a strand of hair from her forehead.

“I didn’t plan to leave.”

She studied him — slow, careful, as if the world had just handed her something fragile and she needed to check for cracks.

Then, quietly:

“This feels more dangerous than any divine realm I’ve stepped into.”

Kai laughed softly. “Because it’s real?”

She nodded.  
 “…Yeah. Because it’s real.”

He kissed her forehead — not a declaration, not a claim, just a soft punctuation mark on the sentence of her fear.

“You don’t have to decide anything today,” he murmured. “You don’t even have to name this yet.”

“And you?” she asked.

“I already know what it is,” he said.

She swallowed. “And you’re… okay with waiting?”

“Waiting,” he said, “is easy. Losing you was the hard part.”

The admission settled between them like warm light, quiet but impossible to ignore.

They separated reluctantly — she to shower, he to make coffee.  
 But the apartment moved with them differently.

Not shy.  
 Not uncertain.

Just aligned.

Hope chirped telepathically once, sending a string of images into Ileana’s mind:

a cup  
 a heart  
 two suns spinning in parallel  
 a nest

Ileana nearly dropped her towel.

“Hope is… uh… broadcasting again,” she called out.

Kai’s voice floated from the kitchen, amused:  
 “She’s learning pictograms.”

“What is the nest supposed to mean?”

A beat.

Then Hope responded:

**home**

Ileana’s breath caught.

She wasn’t ready for that word.  
 But it stayed, ringing in her thoughts, anchoring something she’d always assumed was unanchorable.

Breakfast Became a Ritual

He cooked.  
 She teased him.  
 Hope hummed through the ceiling like a distant radio signal.

The toast burned a little.  
 She scraped it dramatically with a butterknife.  
 He kissed her temple in retaliation.

“I’m not a child,” she said, even as she leaned into him.

“I never said you were.”

“You’re hovering.”

“I’m admiring.”

“You’re impossible.”

“And yet,” he murmured, handing her a plate, “you’re still here.”

She hated how easily that line melted her.

Hope flickered again, sending a feeling that was unmistakably smug.

The Day Played Out Like a Soft Gesture

She studied.  
 He worked.  
 They passed each other in the small space of her apartment like bodies trained to orbit the same sun.

At noon he stretched his arms, groaning.

“You need a break,” Ileana said without looking up.

“Says the woman who typed three pages without blinking.”

“My eyes were open.”

“Barely.”

She stuck her tongue out.  
 He stole her pen.  
 She stole it back.

They lived in the kind of quiet that was once impossible for them.

When her mother called, she answered on speaker — which she never did.  
 Kai was tidying in the kitchen.

Her mother asked about work.  
 About sleep.  
 About food.

Then, casually, in the exact tone that meant she’d been waiting all day to ask:

“And how is Kai?”

Ileana made a strangled sound.  
 Kai froze mid-dish.

“He’s…” she faltered. “He’s around.”

Her mother snorted. “That boy isn’t ‘around.’ That boy is *there*. And he is good for you.”

Kai’s ears went pink.  
 Ileana buried her face in her hands.

“I’m hanging up now,” she muttered.

Her mother laughed all the way to the dial tone.

They didn’t talk about what her mother said.

They didn’t need to.

Instead, they watched a movie in the dark with half their attention, curled together on the couch like something instinctual.

At some point, Hope reached out telepathically — a quiet flicker of feeling.

**together-good**  
 **warm**  
 **stay-close**

Ileana’s throat tightened.

Kai gently squeezed her hand.

“Are you okay?” he whispered.

“Yes,” she said.  
 It came out truer than she expected.  
 “Yes. I think… I am.”

She leaned into him.

Kai breathed out, long and slow, like someone finally allowed to rest.

The Citadel hummed faintly in the distance — respectful, restrained, listening.

And Hope whispered one more thought that neither of them fully understood yet:

**not yet a vow**  
 **but close**

Ileana closed her eyes.

Kai held her a little tighter.

And the night — human, divine, and everything between — settled around them like the slow unfurling of a life finally allowed to begin.

# **CHAPTER XXXV** 

*The First Real Date*

A few days after Anne’s visit, the air in Kai’s apartment felt different.

Not lighter —  
 but opened.

Like someone had turned a page slowly, letting the ink dry.

Ileana was tying her hair by the sink when Kai appeared in the doorway, still in sleep-warm clothes, fingers fidgeting at the hem of his shirt.

She paused.

He never fidgeted.

“Can we… do something today?” he asked carefully.

“Something?” she echoed.

“Something not domestic. Not emotional maintenance. Not crisis control.”  
 He exhaled, steadying himself. “A date.”

Her hands froze mid-motion.

“A… date?”

“Yes.” His voice softened. “A real one. One where we’re allowed to enjoy each other. No apologies. No emotional surgery. Just—”  
 He hesitated, then finished with something almost shy:  
 “us.”

She wasn’t expecting the rush of warmth that hit her.

“I thought you’d want more time,” she whispered.

“I think we’ve had enough time,” he said.  
 “But not enough joy.”

Her heart made a small, startled movement inside her chest.

“…okay,” she said. “A date.”

His eyes brightened — subtly, but unmistakably.

“Good. Dress comfortable. We’re starting early.”

Behind him, a soft pulse of gold flickered in the ceiling corner.

**Hope.**

Then a thin streak of cool silver:

**The Entity.**

A thought brushed the edge of Kai’s perception, like a stern finger tapped lightly on a shoulder:

*Let them have this day.*

Hope dimmed herself obediently.

The world exhaled.

The flea market was a living organism — half-awake vendors, tarps flapping like eyelids, stalls rearranging themselves with the chaotic logic of dreams. People milled around clutching iced coffees, tote bags, armfuls of unwise purchases.

He relaxed here.  
 She noticed instantly.

Maybe because chaos camouflaged him.  
 Maybe because nothing in this place demanded emotional fluency.

And then they passed the stationery stall.

Imported fountain pen inks.  
 Handmade wax seals.  
 Washi tape in soft pastel palettes.  
 Artisan notebooks with deckled edges.  
 Limited-edition brass rulers.

Kai slowed.

Not dramatically — just a perceptible stutter in his gait.

Ileana smirked.  
 “You’re resisting.”

“I am doing no such thing.”

“You are. You’re pretending that a pad of handmade cotton paper isn’t calling your soul.”

He bristled.  
 “I have taste. Not addiction.”

She raised a brow.  
 “Forty-seven letters, Kai. Forty-seven. The paper quality increased with your feelings.”

He went very still.  
 “…I don’t know what you’re talking about.”

She laughed and walked ahead.

He lasted five seconds before peeling off to the stall.

So did she.

The vendor—a wiry man with ink-stained fingers—beamed. “Artists?”

Kai opened his mouth. “I—”

“Yes,” she answered first, smiling lightly, “we used to be.”

Kai shot her a look.  
 A soft, almost shy one.

He picked up a leather-bound journal with a stitched spine and creamy deckled pages. She watched him thumb it like he was reading the paper grain the way one reads intention.

“You like that one?” she asked.

“It’s… good.”

“That means he wants it,” the vendor translated cheerfully.

Kai glared.

Then Ileana picked up a notebook—soft cream, linen cover—and ran her thumb down the binding.

It was the exact type she used to fill when she was younger: journals full of song fragments, sketches, letters she never sent.

He noticed.

Subtly.  
 Immediately.  
 Like instinct.

“You’d fill that in a week,” he murmured.

She blinked. “What?”

“You always wrote fast when you were honest,” he said. “Like your hands were catching up to a thought that had already finished.”

Her breath caught—small, almost invisible.  
 “You remember that?”

His answer was quiet.  
 “You taught me how to write.”

The vendor pretended not to listen but absolutely listened.

Ileana looked away.

Kai cleared his throat, picked up a set of brass pen rests and a bottle of ink in a deep river-blue.  
 “For you,” he said too casually.

“For… me?”

“You journal,” he said. “And I have too many pens. So you pick one of mine, I’ll ink it for you.”

She fought a smile. Lost.  
 “You’re just trying to convert me to the cult of fancy paper.”

“Absolutely,” he said without shame. “Resistance is futile.”

She rolled her eyes, but her hands lingered on the journal.  
 “And this is for you?”

“And for you,” he corrected.  
 “You’ll end up stealing half my notebooks anyway.”

The vendor snorted, delighted.  
 “Two artists in denial,” he announced, ringing up their items.

They left the stall with a bag that contained:

one journal for him  
 one journal for her  
 one shared bottle of ink  
 and the soft, blooming warmth of a habit they used to share.

The vinyl stall was tucked between a spice seller and a stall overflowing with vintage lamps. A record spun on a turntable, fuzzy jazz drifting through the air like nostalgia someone left playing.

Kai went still.

Ileana knew this look—the look of a man meeting a memory.

He flipped through crates with reverent fingers, and then—

He stopped.

She recognized the album cover before he even lifted it.

Her playlist.  
 Her seventeenth birthday gift to him.

She had made a mix of songs—half hopeful, half aching—because she didn’t know how else to say she cared.

He lifted the vinyl like it was something sacred.  
 “I used to listen to this every night,” he murmured. “On the bus. On the way home. In my room. When things were… hard.”

Her chest tightened.  
 “You found the songs I liked and you found versions of yourself inside them,” she whispered.

He nodded slowly.  
 “It got me through.”

She swallowed.  
 “And now it’s here.”

“Yes,” he said. “Still here. Still mine.”

She brushed a thumb along the corner of the sleeve.  
 “This feels like the universe giving something back.”

He didn’t look at the album when he said softly, “You gave it to me first.”

Her heart lurched.

He bought it before she could say anything else.

The steam rose in fragrant curls from the bamboo baskets. The vendor handed them a tray—pillowy dumplings arranged like soft moons.

They sat on a low wooden bench beneath a tarp that flapped every few minutes like a restless curtain. Musikero kids played guitar nearby; someone bartered loudly with a fish vendor; a toddler toddled aggressively at a rooster.

A perfect kind of chaos.

Ileana waited for hers to cool.

Kai did not.

He bit into one immediately.

A jet of steam shot into his mouth.

The sound he made defied taxonomy.  
 Somewhere between warrior grunt, dying bird, and a man trying desperately to save face.

She nearly dropped her own dumpling.  
 “Are you okay?”

“…Yes,” he said through clenched teeth.

“You’re crying.”

“It’s the steam.”

“Do you want water?”

“No.”

“Do you want dignity?”

“That left my body fifteen seconds ago.”

She laughed so hard she had to put her dumpling down.

He attempted to glare but his eyes were watering, which ruined the effect entirely.

She reached over without thinking and wiped a flour smudge from his cheek.

He stilled.

Just for a moment.

Then she cleared her throat and looked away, suddenly warm.

“Eat slowly,” she scolded softly.

“Yes,” he murmured, “Mom.”

She threw a napkin at him.

It was small—just a pop-up tent with easels, battered stools, and a paint-splattered table. Someone had written on a cardboard sign:

**“Paint each other. 10 minutes. No perfection allowed.”**

The artist running the booth—a woman with paint-streaked overalls and a kind, mischievous smile—gestured them in.

“Couple?”

“No,” Ileana said.

“Yes,” Kai said at the same time.

The artist grinned. “Perfect.”

They sat facing each other, knees almost touching.

Brushes.  
 Small canvas boards.  
 A decade of shared memory suddenly sitting between them like a third chair.

The artist set a timer.

“Ten minutes,” she said. “And no erasing.”

They looked at each other.

Something old flickered.

She — the kind who could do everything: paint, music, literature, craft, design.  
 A prodigy in all directions.

He — the boy with graphite-stained fingers, the one who could coax entire worlds out of pencil and shadow until her words taught him how to make worlds with language too.

She dipped her brush, squinting at him.  
 “You still scrunch your eyebrows when you concentrate.”

“You still tilt your head too much. You’re going to get neck pain.”

“You’re obnoxious.”

“You’re avoiding the fact that you look at me like I’m a still life.”

“I’m trying to draw your face, idiot.”

“You never had trouble drawing my face.”

She faltered.

He caught it—quietly, tenderly.

“You don’t have to be who you were,” he said softly.  
 “I just like looking at you now.”

Her brush slowed.

His did too.

The timer ticked down.

They painted each other in silence—not the silence of strangers, but the silence of two people who once spoke fluently in art.

When the timer rang, they set their canvases down.

Her painting of him was soft, warm around the edges—like she had painted the version of him she trusted, not the version he showed the world.

His painting of her was gentle and unguarded—like he had painted the truth he saw even when she tried to hide.

The artist nodded approvingly.

“People who paint each other kindly,” she said, “don’t stay strangers for long.”

Ileana flushed.  
 Kai looked at her like he agreed.

They carried the paintings with them, side by side.

And the day felt like it was quietly, inevitably shifting into something larger than either of them had planned.

The museum doors slid open into a cool hush — the kind that swallowed footsteps and turned breath into something deliberate. Evening hours meant the halls were quieter, the lights dimmer, the world softened at the edges.

They stepped inside, and immediately she drifted toward a display with the instinct of someone who had spent years learning how to see.

Kai followed.

Not because of the art.

Because of her.

She paused in front of a painting — a storm of color and form, abstract enough to invite projection, bold enough to resist interpretation. She tilted her head in that familiar way, the way she did when analyzing a chord progression or a stubborn stanza.

He felt something warm unfurl in his chest.

“You still do that,” he murmured.

“Do what?” she asked without looking away.

“Look at things like you’re reading them.”

Her lips twitched.  
 “You used to make fun of me for that.”

He stepped closer — not touching, but near enough she felt him.  
 “I didn’t know what I was looking at,” he said softly.  
 “But I do now.”

She finally glanced at him, a faint question in her eyes.  
 He didn’t elaborate.  
 He didn’t need to.

They moved through the galleries — slow, thoughtful — their footfalls synced without effort. She paused at every sculpture with an almost reverent curiosity, tracing the air around it with her fingers.

Kai watched her like a man memorizing constellations.

He had always loved art through her — even before he understood why.

At the Impressionists section, she stopped in front of a Monet.

“I used to love these,” she said. “Soft edges, blurry gardens, easy beauty.”

“And now?” he asked.

“I still like them,” she admitted. “But they feel… distant. Like someone remembering happiness instead of feeling it.”

Kai stared at her.  
 Not at the painting.  
 Not at the brushstrokes.  
 At her.

There were whole museums in her face:  
 the softness at the corner of her mouth when she thought,  
 the faint lines of exhaustion and devotion,  
 the unguarded vulnerability she let slip only when she forgot to pretend.

“You’re more beautiful than Monet,” he said before he could stop himself.

She blinked.  
 “What?”

He swallowed.  
 Ducked his head slightly.  
 “I didn’t say that out loud,” he muttered.

“You did.”

“Well,” he said, embarrassed, “it’s true.”

She looked stunned — not because it was romantic, but because it was so Kai: blunt, unembellished, delivered like a fact.

“You can’t just say things like that,” she whispered.

“Why?” he asked, genuine.

“Because,” she said, voice tightening, “I’m not… I’m not painting anymore. I’m not writing. I don’t look like a muse. I’m tired. I’m—”

“Beautiful,” he said again, firmer this time, like he was correcting an error in a graph.

She opened her mouth, but he gently cut her off.

“And Da Vinci would’ve hated you.”

“What?” she sputtered.

“You’d distract him,” he said simply. “He’d get jealous of his own paintings.”

She almost choked on her breath.

Kai stared at her — serious, almost clinical — as if he had footnotes prepared to defend the statement.

She laughed — soft, incredulous, flickering — and the sound filled the gallery like a living thing.

They moved on.

She drifted toward a sculpture — a marble figure mid-reach, stone impossibly fluid. She stepped closer, absorbing details with artist-trained eyes.

Kai didn’t look at the piece.

He studied her profile, the soft concentration, the faint crease between her brows, the way her fingers hovered but didn’t touch.

He whispered, under his breath:

“She’s art.”

She turned slightly.  
 “What?”

“Nothing,” he said quickly.

They lingered until the announcement chimed:

**The museum will be closing in fifteen minutes.**

But neither moved immediately.

Instead, they stood beside each other, watching the last visitors drift away — two artists rediscovering a language they had once spoken fluently.

She finally exhaled, soft.  
 “I missed this,” she said.

“The museum?” he asked.

She shook her head.  
 “You.”

He looked at her.

And in the dim gallery glow, something shifted — quiet, certain, inevitable.

He didn’t touch her.  
 But he wanted to.  
 He didn’t kiss her.  
 But he could have.

“Come on,” he whispered, offering his hand.  
 “We have one more stop.”

She took it.

Their fingers slipped together naturally, like memory.

Outside, the last trace of sunset clung to the sky like a held breath.

Kai rented two bikes with suspicious efficiency.

Ileana fiddled with her helmet, failing terribly.

Kai stepped in, hands gentle, brushing her jaw as he fixed the buckle.

Her breath caught.

He cleared his throat. “Ready?”

“…Yeah.”

They pushed off.

The city blurred — neon signs, restaurant chatter, streetlamps warming into gold halos.

Ileana sped ahead like a challenge.  
 Kai yelled behind her:

“Ileana\! Slow down—”

“You slow down\!”

“You’re going to hit a parked car\!”

“That’s your fault\!”

He chased her through winding streets, their laughter peeling behind them like ribbon.

She hadn’t felt weightless in years.

The shop was tiny, squeezed between a flower stall and a bakery. Soft yellow lights glowed in the windows.

The bell chimed.

It smelled like paper and memory.

Kai went straight to philosophy.

She drifted to fiction.

He peeked over a paperback.  
 “You’re choosing at least three books.”

“I’m choosing one.”

“You’re choosing three,” he repeated, grabbing Kierkegaard, “because you always say one and walk out with a small library.”

“That’s not— ok, maybe.”

He smiled.

Soon, they walked to the counter:

He carried a slim, serious volume.  
 She carried three soft-spined paperbacks.

They paid separately.

Outside, Kai simply took her bag of books and slipped it into his backpack.

“Hey—”

“It’s safer with mine,” he said.

She opened her mouth to argue—  
 closed it—  
 smiled.

The building’s elevator rattled all the way up like it was doing its best on old bones.  
 Ileana snorted. “You definitely didn’t ask permission.”

Kai pushed his glasses up the bridge of his nose. “I did.”

“And did the guard actually *say* yes?”

Kai hesitated.

“…He admired my glasses.”

“So you bribed him with charm.”

“That is a very generous interpretation of my social skillset.”

She laughed as the doors opened.

The rooftop was wide, empty, washed in the glow of the city’s gold-lit pulse. The air was cool, brushing past them like something trying to be gentle.

“Oh,” she breathed, stepping toward the railing. “It’s beautiful.”

Kai wasn’t looking at the skyline.  
 “Yes,” he murmured.

She turned and caught him staring. He didn’t even pretend otherwise.

“Kai.”

He smiled — small, shy, sincere.  
 “Stay there.”

She blinked. “What?”

“Don’t move.”

He lifted his phone and started taking photos.  
 Her hair stirring in the wind.  
 Her cheeks tinted a soft evening pink.  
 Her laugh as she hid her face.  
 Her stubborn turn-away.  
 Her reluctant turn-back.

“You’re absurd,” she said, warmth slipping into her voice.

“You’re beautiful,” he corrected.

This time she didn’t tell him to stop.

After far too many pictures, she tugged his phone out of his hands.

“My turn.”

She photographed him in ways that made something in his chest go soft:

Him with the wind pulling his shirt.  
 Him adjusting his glasses.  
 Him gazing at the skyline like he could read it.  
 Him smiling — real, unguarded.  
 Him looking at her as if she were the thing worth photographing.

When she lowered the phone, the air hummed — quiet, molten, waiting.

“Send them to me,” he said.

“Fine.”

“And to the group chat.”

“Kai—”

“And to your parents.”

“KAI.”

But he was already typing.

Within seconds, the photos were sent to:  
 Alex  
 Marie  
 Nics  
 Anne  
 Her mother  
 Her father  
 The group chat  
 And himself

Her phone buzzed violently.

**Alex:** is this a soft launch  
 **Nics:** THIS IS AN OFFICIAL LAUNCH WTF  
 **Marie:** you look beautiful oh my gosh  
 **Anne:** I’m glad you’re both out together.

And from her mother:

**Mom:** Who took the picture of you two together? He's good.

She flushed.  
 “Give me that,” she said, reaching for his phone.

He lifted it above her head.  
 “No.”

“Stop being tall.”

“No.”

“I hate you.”

“You don’t.”

The movement brought them too close — breath to breath, almost touching.

His voice dropped.  
 “May I?”

She didn’t answer.

She kissed him.

Not carefully.  
 Not cautiously.  
 With the quiet confidence of someone who had waited years to stop trembling.

He matched her — one hand at her waist, the other gentle at the base of her neck.  
 She rose on her toes.  
 He bent to meet her halfway.

The kiss eased into something slow, warm, sure.  
 Not a peak.  
 Not a promise.  
 A beginning.

When they parted, foreheads touching, she whispered:

“I liked today.”

He whispered back:

“I liked you today.”

A beat.

“I like you every day.”

She kissed him again.  
 Soft, certain.

They held hands all the way back, fingers laced without thinking.

The city felt quieter, like it was making room for them.

Halfway down the block, she nudged him, voice shy but teasing:

“So… we’re a family now, huh?”

Kai nearly tripped over a crack in the sidewalk.  
 “I— Hope has… unconventional taxonomy.”

She snorted.  
 “Understatement. She called us Mom and Dad. That’s not taxonomy. That’s destiny.”

He groaned into his scarf.  
 “I did not plan for this.”

“No one ever plans for a godling,” she said, softer. “But it wasn’t bad.”

His head turned slightly.  
 “Not bad?”

She exhaled, breath fogging in the cool air.

“It’s… strange. But in the good way. The… ‘I’m not alone in the universe’ way.”

He went still beside her.

“When things are good,” she said softly, “I’m always waiting for them to break. Or vanish. Or turn out to be tricks I fell for like a fool.”

His hand tightened around hers — instinct, not intention.

“But now?” she continued. “With you. And Hope. And your cosmic… uncle… whatever he is—”

She laughed under her breath, then sobered.

“It feels like the floor holds. Like nothing’s waiting to collapse under me.”

He swallowed hard.

“And it’s not because of divinity,” she added quickly. “It’s not about realms or titles or all the impossible things stitched into your bones.”

Her eyes lifted — steady, warm, unguarded.

“It’s because it’s you.”

He stopped walking.

For a moment he just stared, breath caught between wonder and something heartbreakingly tender.

“You make it feel like I’m allowed to stay,” she whispered. “That’s new.”

He lifted her hand and pressed it to his lips.  
 Once.  
 Soft.  
 Reverent.

“I want you here,” he said, voice quiet but unshaking. “That’s all I’ve ever wanted.”

She leaned against him, shoulder to shoulder, a small gravity pulling them close.

“Then I think…” she murmured, “I like this family.”

A breath.  
 A laugh.  
 His hand finding her waist.

“Me too.”

Later that night, they lay in her bed — not touching at first, just facing each other in the dim light of her bedside lamp.

Her hair was still damp from her shower.  
 His glasses were off, making his face look younger, softer.

She shifted closer, the blanket rustling.

“Can I—?” she whispered.

He opened his arm.

She moved into him, head against his chest, hand over his heartbeat.  
 Like it was the most natural place in the world.

“Good day,” she murmured.

“The best,” he said.

“Thank you.”

“For what?”

“For…”  
 She hesitated.  
 Then:  
 “For making it feel easy.”

He pressed a kiss into her nose.

For a moment, the only sound was their breathing syncing.

Then—

Soft.  
 Unplanned.  
 True.

“I love you,” she whispered.

He didn’t jolt.  
 He didn’t freeze.  
 He didn’t hesitate.

He tucked her closer, voice dissolving into her hair:

“I love you too.”

No thunder.  
 No mythic surge.  
 Just two people in the dark, holding each other as if they’d finally reached the same page.

And outside the window, the city lights flickered —  
 not a blessing,  
 not a miracle,  
 just a quiet acknowledgment:

The page had turned.  
 And the story had deepened.
